And **`Token` had to shrink to grow**: it is embedded twice per `TokenSource`, once per `EvalOp`, in a 768-deep static op stack, so four bytes on it cost **6,144 B of `bss`** on a board at 95.6 %. Narrowing `type`/`length` to absorb it was measured and rejected — 16 % on the loop, 8 % on a frame, leaving the loop slower than M1. What paid instead was deleting `NodeIterator`'s `has_peeked`/`peeked_token`, a lookahead buffer nothing ever set to true: **`pico2` RAM 95.62 % → 93.86 %, 9,208 bytes returned**, free nodes down only 194/258 per game, all three presets link. 6 tests added to `test_primitives_procedures.c`, mostly running the call from inside a list because that is the only path the cache serves: a slot freed by `erase` and refilled by the next definition, a name defined after resolving to "neither", `erall`, a `copydef` alias, the plain repeated call, and redefinition through both the lexer and the cached path. 67/67 ctest green |
| 2026-08-01 | P10 | M2 measured on hardware, and **M3 decided: P10 stops, M4 declined**. M2 did what it was designed to do and nothing the games could collect. On a Pico 2 a user-procedure call stopped scaling with workspace size — full table **128.3 → 24.0 µs (5.35×)**, small workspace 32.6 → 24.4, spread 3.94× → **0.98×** — which removes a cliff every growing Logo program was walking towards. But Turtle Trails' `play.frame` went 73.2 → **73.4 ms** and Checkpoint Run's 232.7 → **232.6 ms**, both flat against a host that predicted 1.17× for each, and the profiled loop **regressed 1.64×** (65.9 → 108.1 µs/iter), with `draw.board` and `draw.sector` slipping 3–6 %. **So all of P10's game-visible gain is M1's**: Trails 87.3 → 73.4 ms (1.19×), Checkpoint Run 258.6 → 232.6 ms (1.11×), against a 40 ms target that is **not met**. M3 therefore declines M4: it targets the cons-cell walk, which is the same cost P9's C map removes outright from `tile.at` (59 % of a Trails frame, 43 of the 73.4 ms — a frame near 30 ms without it), for far more risk and a GC-touching change. **The loop regression is unexplained and is the one live lead.** M2 added no work to that loop — it resolves one name per iteration, and a nine-step binary search plus a re-intern became a single memo read — so nothing algorithmic accounts for 42 µs an iteration. The host does not reproduce it: host:board on that loop went 77× at M1 to **135× at M2**, a 1.76× board-only penalty. That shape points at instruction fetch (the RP2350 runs from external flash through the XIP cache, and M2 moved and grew the hot path) rather than at the algorithm. Concrete lead on the record: `pico2` reports **XIP_RAM 0 B of 16 KB, 0 % used**, and the SDK can place chosen functions there with `__not_in_flash_func` — the token and resolution path is the obvious candidate, and it would lift M1's gains too. Hypothesis only; measuring it needs a board. Whether M2 stays is finely balanced and recorded in design §6.4: for it, the scan cliff is gone, 9 KB of SRAM came back, and the games are neutral rather than worse; against it, token-heavy code that calls few procedures is 1.64× slower on the board and the games gained nothing. Recommendation: keep, and chase the XIP_RAM lead |

Cost: `pico2` RAM 93.86 % → **93.94 %**, +~380 B `bss` — a 32-byte slot-filled bitmap and the 320-byte row buffer `stampmap` bakes through (`TILEMAP_ROW_MAX`); all three presets link, the host REPL runs the storage half with the graphics primitives as silent no-ops. The one risk it cannot cover on a Plus 2 W: that board takes the PSRAM tier, so the SRAM tier's 8 KB **heap** allocation on a Pico 2 at 93.94 % RAM is still unproven. **Next: M3**, the Turtle Trails render revamp — the map replaces `tile.at`'s cons walk inside `step.bugs` (43 of the frame's 73.4 ms) and `stampmap` replaces the 5,916 ms `draw.board` |

| 2026-08-02 | P9 | M3 done: **Turtle Trails revamped in place** (design §13.3) — render-only, gameplay byte-identical, and the game's own storage of the board replaced. A map cell now holds a **bank slot**, which has to answer both "what does this look like" and "what is this", so the slots are ordered to make every rule one comparison: 0 off the board, 1 hedge, 2 nest floor *and* door, 3..18 open path (one variant per walkable-neighbour mask), 19..34 the same carrying a speck, 35..50 the same carrying a blossom — `walk?` is `> 2`, `nest.open?` is `> 1`, paintable is `>= 19`, and painting subtracts 16 or 32 to reach the plain variant of the same shape. Two pairs of §5.2 codes therefore share a picture (tunnel with empty corridor, nest floor with its door); nothing in the game ever told them apart. **The mask is the rounded corner**, so the 50 tiles are drawn with the pen the maze used to be carved with: a fat hedge dot, then a pen-8 stroke run into each walkable neighbour — a stroke that stops at the cell centre leaves a round cap, one that runs on leaves a square edge. `setup.tiles` draws them once a session and `draw.board` becomes `stampmap`. One pixel changes and it is not a bug: a pen-8 disc is *nine* pixels across, so the carved board had nine-pixel corridors eating a pixel off the hedge either side, and tiles make corridors eight and one-cell walls eight rather than six — visibly the same board, not pixel-identical, and no tile scheme could be, since the bleed makes a cell depend on its neighbours' neighbours. The map is **40 × 77**: a bake starts at the top left of the graphics area, so it must be the whole screen with the 28×36 board at cell offset (6, 2) — which also lets `tile.at` drop its four bounds tests, because a step off the board lands on margin and reads 0 — and rows 41–76 hold the finished board off the bottom of the 608-pixel world where the sampler never reaches, so `reset.board` copies them down at a level start (two primitives a cell against the twenty deriving one costs). Deleted: `decode.map`, `tile.code`, `count.paint`, `fill.board`, `carve.paths`, `sweep.row`/`sweep.col`, `mark.tile`, `draw.specks`, and the ~1,050-cell nested Logo map. **Numbers** (new `trails.board` line in `test_bench_throughput`, host, same machine): level build **56.7 → 3.3 ms**, 17×, of which the bake is 0.07 ms; `play.frame` unmoved at ~0.61 ms, as design §3.4 predicted — the tile system does not touch the simulation. Tests: map invariants (symmetry, connectivity, sealed nest, no dead ends, tunnels) now read the **encoded words** rather than the runtime map, since that is the source data; three new tests pin what the map became — the built map's slot against the letter *and* the neighbour mask for all 1,008 cells, the kept copy against the live board across a level change, a bank tile's strokes against every one of the 16 masks, the bake's 224×288 landing on a 320×320 screen with nothing outside it, and a one-cell `stamptile` repair hitting exactly its 8×8. 69/69 ctest green. **Open: the hardware run** — the Pico 2's 5,916 ms against the new level build, and whether the frame moves once `tile.at`'s 36+28 cons walk inside `step.bugs` is an index; `p9m0.trails` now prints `setup.level` beside `draw.board` for it |

Two consequences worth carrying forward. **Turtle Trails' 40 ms target has no named lever left**: what is in the frame is ordinary interpreter overhead spread thin, the target of P10's declined M4 and its rejected bytecode body, both of which were rejected partly *because* P9's map was believed decisive — reopening either is a fresh decision. **Board caveat, found 2026-08-02:** these runs are on a **Pico Plus 2 W** while every baseline they are set against (5,916 ms, 87.3 ms, 73.4 ms) is a **Pico 2** — same RP2350 and clock, different flash part, and instruction fetch is precisely the open lead in both designs. The two M3 runs are comparable with each other, so the B11 and `tile.at` findings hold; the cross-board pairs do not establish what they were written to. Design §13.5 has the same-board re-run (the pre-M3 game is still in git; load it as `trailsold` and run `p9m0.trails` on the same board for a real *before*), and a **Pico 2** run is worth more than another Plus 2 W one, since its SRAM tier has still never allocated. The confirmation is the total: a host frame is 0.615 ms against a 781 ns benchmark iteration, so **787 operations predicted against 791 measured** — identical composition on both machines, the board uniformly ~131× slower per operation, and nothing board-specific hiding in it. **The lever is the calibration pair.** A `make "x (:x + 1)` costs **102.5 µs against a procedure call's 24 µs — 4.3×**, where the host's own two numbers give 2.5×; both ratios are within one machine, so unlike the frame figures the comparison is sound. Calls scale host→board at 75×, a `make` with arithmetic at 129×. M2 already made calls cheap, so what is left is the statement, and the hot slots are almost nothing but `make` statements (`place.all` is fourteen per actor, five times a frame). The piece inside a `make` that is not inside a call is **variable resolution** — the one name lookup §3.2/§7 left uncached as "dynamically scoped", which is a reason it cannot use M2's atom memo, not a reason it must stay slow. Five runs reproduce every slot within 2 % (FRAME 80.99-81.15 ms). They priced the pieces, net of a 4 µs bare loop: **user procedure call 17.5 µs, variable read 37, variable write 48, `(1 + 1)` 67.5, the whole statement 97.5**. So **a variable write costs 2.7× a procedure call** — M2 spent itself on calls and got them cheap; variable access, the one lookup §3.2/§7 left uncached as dynamically scoped, is now the dearest elementary operation and the hot slots are made of it. **A game-side saving fell out on the way**: a call's last argument absorbs the whole expression, so the outermost parens of `make "v (…)`, `output (…)` and `.setitem i :l (…)` are redundant — and cost 18 % of such a statement on the host. Sixty came out of `logo/games/trails` (expression-form only; `(name …)` may be a varargs call whose bare arity differs), measured at **2.8 % off `place.all` and 2.6 % off `step.bugs`**, full suite green including the bit-exact simulation and the soak. The game's header now says not to add them by habit. **Number literals were suspected and cleared**: `ignore (1 + 1)` 73.5 µs against `ignore (:x + :x)` 90.5 — the variable form is dearer, so §4.1's dropped numeric cache stays dropped. That pair also prices an operand twice over, 8.5 µs for a variable reference over a literal from two independent measurements agreeing to a tenth of a microsecond. I read the remaining ~32 µs as the grouping paren plus the infix operator; **the host disproved that** (new `test_bench_expr_shapes`, same interpreter, net of a 69 ns bare loop): `ignore (1 + 1)` 606 ns is 55 ns *cheaper* than `ignore sum 1 1` 587+, so infix beats a prefix primitive, as it should. The only removable overhead is the **grouping paren**, ~11 % of an expression and **~15-20 % of a `make "v (expr)`** — a game-side saving available today, since the outermost parens are redundant when the last argument absorbs the expression anyway. **The real lead is a ratio**: board:host is ~65× for a bare loop and ~68× for a procedure call, but **135× for an arithmetic statement** — the board is twice as bad at arithmetic statements as at everything else, and the shape data says it is not the operator, the operands or the literals. Prime suspect is instruction fetch on a code path entered once per statement, the same story as §2.2's 34 %-on-board word classification and P9 §13.2. The presets' idle `XIP_RAM: 0 B of 16 KB` is **not** the way in — that region is the XIP cache reconfigured as RAM, offered only for binaries not executing from flash, and Pico Logo does. **The experiment was built and it worked (design §11.2-§11.3)**: new `core/hot.h` gives `__not_in_flash_func` a host-safe spelling (`LOGO_HOT`, the shim P9 §13.2 also wanted for the tilemap sampler), carried by `eval_primary`, `token_source_next`, `eval_expr_bp` and `step_expr_eval` — 6.4 KB of the interpreter's hottest code. Off by default; `-DLOGO_HOT_IN_RAM=ON` costs **+6,144 B on `pico2`, 93.94 → 95.11 %** (25.6 KB still free), all three presets link, symbols verified moving from flash to SRAM and back. **Measured 2026-08-04 on a Plus 2 W: the frame went 81.0 → 65.5 ms, 1.24×, and the 212× paren-call path collapsed to 38× — below the 60× a bare `repeat` iteration costs.** `sync` was unmoved at 1.74 ms, the control that rules out drift. Instruction fetch is no longer a hypothesis. Two further findings: the code *left* in flash got worse (a procedure call 17 → 22 µs, ratio 67 → 87×) because moving 6 KB reshuffles the layout, so the boundary is arbitrary and whatever stays behind pays; and a second tier followed — the call path, `var_get`/`var_set` (the dearest elementary operation) and the `mem_car`/`mem_cdr` accessors §3.3 named at 20 % — for 3.6 KB more, built and unmeasured. RAM: `pico2` 93.94 → 95.11 % (tier 1) → **95.90 %** (both), 21 KB free — which turned out to be a 108 KB op stack left at 768 from the single-board era, not the board, so the option is on for `pico2w` and `pico+2w` and off for `pico2` only for want of hardware to boot it on. §1's 40 ms now needs 1.64× more, and the question has changed shape: how much of the interpreter fits in RAM, rather than which rejected rewrite to reopen. Both runs reproduce every slot within 2 %. 40 ms from 81 is 2.03×. Two smaller results: **`sync` is 1.64 ms**, the first honest in-frame present this project has measured, so the display is not the problem for a game that dirties only its sprites; and the first run lost the tail of its own report to a `;` inside a bracketed list — the hazard `tests/logo/p9m2` records — which now fails at load in `test_trails.c`, mutation-checked, for every Logo file it reads |

It is also why `pico2` reads as tighter than `pico2w`, which carries a WiFi stack and still links smaller. So the option is **on for `pico2w` (89.36 %, 55 KB free) and `pico+2w` (91.05 %, 46 KB)** — the measured board, and one strictly less pressured than it — and off for `pico2`, for want of hardware to boot rather than evidence against. **Tier 2 then measured at 53.165 ms** (design §11.4) — another 1.23×, **81.0 → 53.2 cumulative, 1.52×**, `sync` 1.775 ms across all three runs as the control. Built, unmeasured. The frame is also now improving more slowly than the calibration statement (×0.811 against ×0.734), which says what is left is loop overhead and primitive bodies rather than statement evaluation. **Tier 3 then measured at 48.095 ms** (design §11.5) — 1.105×, **81.0 → 48.1 cumulative, 1.68×**, `sync` 1.76 ms over four runs. Neither game called `recycle` anywhere (the Invaders design claimed one in a `march.step` that does not exist), so a long scoring session still slid towards `out of space`: both now call Trails' `reclaim` every 250 frames. New `tests/test_invaders.c` (11 tests — the game had none of its own, only the shared HUD test) and three more in `test_galaxian.c` pin the free frame, the cost of a scoring one, and that the timer holds free storage flat; the soak steps the mock clock a frame at a time so the collision demons actually poll (a stopped clock suppresses every poll after the first, `DEMON_POLL_MS`). Two cold-start defects fell out of it (`docs/bugs.md`, 2026-08-06). Metrics now go to files as well as screens — `bench-throughput.txt` in the build directory, `p10games.txt` on the board — because a number you can only read off a display cannot be pasted into a design doc. 70/70 green. **Not applied: the tile map.** P9 M3's win was the static board (5,916 → 7.6 ms), and the C map moved the Trails *frame* by 0.2 ms; a marching formation on a 20×22 px pitch stepping 4 px would have to be re-gridded to 8-px tiles and 8-px steps — a change of feel for no measured gain. Still open from the Trails list: the wall-clock `setspeed` glide against per-frame tuning constants (`attack.turn`, `peel.frames`, `fire.chance` are per *frame*, motion is per *second*, so the dive geometry is silently frame-rate dependent), the eight `when` demons against Trails' explicit collision pass, and idle-path statement counts |

| 2026-08-11 | Editor | Incremental search in the full-screen editor, the last unimplemented key in the reference's editor chapter (user request). `Ctrl` `F` starts it; typing extends the search text and jumps to the first match at or after the current position, `↓`/`↑` cycle forward and backward through every occurrence (both wrap), `←Back` widens the search back out, and the match is selected as block editing already selects — so `Ctrl` `X`/`C`/`V` and the indent keys work on it the moment `Esc` leaves the search. The footer carries the typed text left-justified in reverse video, up to its 40-column width. **The documented key could not be used**: `Ctrl` `I` is `0x09`, which is exactly `KEY_TAB`, because `keyboard_poll` maps `Ctrl`+letter as `ch &= 0x1F`; binding it would have cost the documented `Tab` tab-stop insert, so the reference now says `Ctrl` `F` (`0x06`, unused). Matching is case-insensitive — Logo's own names are, and `fd` should find `FD`; with no match anywhere the selection is dropped and the cursor returns to the last match, which is what makes `←Back` symmetric. The search itself is a pure wrapping matcher in `devices/picocalc/editor_search.c` so it is testable on the host without mocking the LCD (new `test_editor_search.c`, 15 tests, the pattern `dirty_tiles`/`costumes` already use); the ~40 lines of state and key handling in `editor.c` remain board-only. 71/71 green, all four presets build |
| 2026-08-11 | Editor | **Replace, on top of the incremental search** (user request). `Ctrl` `R` during a search turns the footer into a `Replace:` prompt; `Enter` replaces every match in the buffer and leaves the search, `Esc` abandons the replacement and drops back into it with the match still selected. The footer now names which prompt it is — `Search: ` and `Replace:`, both padded to 8 columns so the text starts in the same place — which is what set the field at **32 characters** rather than the 40 the search shipped with. The replacement is a *typed field*, not the search's append-only text: `←`/`→` move through it, `←Back` erases to the left and `Del` at the cursor, and `Tab` (`0x09`) falls out as ignored along with every other non-printable, since the field takes `0x20`–`0x7E` only. **The cursor had to leave the content area**, and a block cursor in the footer would have been invisible: `lcd_draw_cursor`'s block branch draws the cell's background on a fixed `TXT_WHITE`, which is *exactly* what the reverse-video footer already looks like, so the replacement field uses the underline cursor packed as the editor background on white. Its one soft edge is a full 32-character field, where the last insert point would sit in column 40 and is clamped to 39, under the last character. Replacement is case-insensitive in what it matches and verbatim in what it writes (`fd` → `forward` also rewrites `FD`), and matches do not overlap — `aa` in `aaaa` is two replacements, and a replacement containing the search text is never matched again, so `fd` → `fd fd` terminates. `editor_search_replace_all` **counts the matches before it moves a byte** and refuses the whole rewrite if the result would not fit the edit buffer, because a buffer that filled up part way through would leave the text half replaced; an empty replacement is legal and erases every match. It joins the matcher in `editor_search.c`, host-testable without an LCD (13 more tests, 28 in `test_editor_search.c`); the field editing in `editor.c` stays board-only. 71/71 green, all four presets build, `pico+2w` RAM 91.64 → 91.65 %. **Verified on a Pimoroni Pico Plus 2 W**, which is the only part of this that a host test can reach — the footer prompts, the underline cursor in reverse video and the field editing all draw straight to the LCD |

Pure Logo against primitives that already exist; no interpreter or device work proposed. **The design's one open question is how a frame gets erased** (§3) and M0 exists to settle it: erase-in-place (two drawing passes, a present of only the strokes) against clear-and-redraw (one pass, a full-screen present). The eraser is a **pen colour** instead, which is what the HUD erase already does, costs two statements a frame rather than two per rock per pass, and unlike `pe` leaves a trace the mock records (it logs a segment only with the pen `LOGO_PEN_DOWN`), so the retrace test is possible at all. And §12's table counts drawing statements but not the **9-way shape dispatch** that reaches them, which erase-in-place pays 24 times a frame; M0 now measures it separately. The nine outlines are generated by `scripts/gen_rocks.py` from radii at equal angular spacing, carry one decimal place (a single literal token, so it evaluates at an integer's cost, and it holds closure under half a pixel where integer turns drift by two or three), and drop the turn after the final segment since `place` sets the heading every pass — which is what makes the statement counts 19/15/13. New `tests/test_asteroids.c` (8 tests) pins the closure of all nine, that the walk out to the first vertex does not draw, that the dispatch reaches the outline it names, that an erase pass retraces its draw pass segment for segment, the one-pixel pen, and that the script runs end to end and its report reaches the file. **73/73 green.** Also fixed on the way: [B15](bugs.md), the reference describing `sin` as outputting a cosine — which is help text on a board, not just prose. **Not run on hardware**, and that run is the gate: M1 does not start until M0 says which strategy the game is written around |

Twelve rocks: erase-in-place body 50.5 ms + present 21.9 = **72.4**; clear-and-redraw body 25.9 + present 26.3 = **52.1**. The two are level at six rocks and clear wins by 9 ms at nine and 20 at twelve, so erase-in-place never wins during play, and it is the *more* complicated of the two — so §3.2 comes out and takes with it the erase-before-step ordering rule the design called its signature failure mode, the crossing-outline artefact, and the HUD-last constraint §10 was built around. `play.frame` is three procedures shorter than the one designed. **The decisive number is the one §3.3 predicted would decide it**: a 12-rock dirty region is 21.9 ms of a 26.3 ms full screen, **84 %**, against a guess of 9–12 ms. The mechanism is the tracker's row spans — one inclusive span per tile row, so a rock near each end dirties the whole row, and a dozen scattered rocks reach almost every row while their outlines cover a few per cent of the pixels. **Sparsity in pixels does not survive a row-span tracker**, which is the general result: a vector game is a different animal from a sprite game on this display, and Asteroids' present alone is 2.3× Galaxian's entire 11.22 ms frame. **§12's central assumption was also wrong, and instructively**: it bracketed a literal-argument primitive call between a procedure call (21 µs) and an arithmetic statement (48) and took the midpoint, 35 — but a pen-down `fd 17` *rasterises*, so it measures **60 µs**, above the arithmetic statement rather than below it. Every drawing line in the budget was 72 % low. The bracket was sound for a primitive that computes and never applied to one that draws. A third finding was nobody's estimate at all — the **9-way shape dispatch costs 370 µs a rock**, a fifth of the 2.10 ms a rock costs in total, because reaching one of nine outlines is two procedure calls and up to four `if`s each walking an `item`. Outlines go nine → three, one per size (the `rshape` list disappears; rotation already varies how a rock reads in motion). Segments go 8/6/5 → 6/5/4. Every per-frame tuning constant is re-cut by a third, rotation 12° → 16° a frame among them. The worst case then fits by **0.5 ms**, which is not a margin: the physics third of the frame is still estimated and **M1 measures it**, with the reserve lever (segregating rocks into three per-size lists, removing dispatch entirely) held for if it comes in over. **B16, fixed the same morning, is what made the comparison possible** — unfixed, clear-and-redraw flashed the whole screen every frame and lost on appearance rather than on time, and this design would have been written around the slower strategy on the strength of an artefact. It cannot simply be raised for `pico+2w`, because the same firmware must come up on a board whose PSRAM fails `psram_verify()` at boot, and the fallback would then ask `malloc` for 256 KB of an SRAM heap that is 91 % gone. So the size is now chosen at **run time**: one `mem_region_alloc(2 × 256 KB)` for both buffers, or the 24576 SRAM pair if there is no region or it is too full. Taking the pair as **one** block is the point of that call — the bounds are a single `editor_buffer_size`, and a region with room for only the first would leave the edit buffer large and the definition buffer small. **The capacity was the easy half.** `editor_get_line_start`, `editor_get_line_at_pos` and `editor_get_col_at_pos` resolved a line number by counting newlines from the start of the buffer, and one keystroke asks for one of them ~10 times (cursor, h-scroll, dirty redraw), a full redraw once per visible row — so the cost of every edit scaled with **the cursor's offset into the buffer**. At 24 KB that is invisible; at 256 KB, editing near the bottom of a large file is ~10 × 250 KB of PSRAM scanning per keystroke, and a `DIRTY_FROM_LINE` redraw multiplies it by thirty. They now go through `editor_lines.c`: a memo of the last `(line, offset)` resolved, scanned forward or backward from, which is **kept across an edit at or after the memoised line** — text before an edit does not move, and the view anchor is almost always above the cursor — so typing costs a few bytes of scan rather than a quarter of a megabyte. The three insert/delete loops that shifted the buffer a byte at a time became `memmove` while they were being touched. **The memo is only correct if every mutation declares itself**, so `editor_lines_edit` is called at all eight sites that move bytes and `editor_lines_reset` at the two that rewrite wholesale (replace-all, editor entry); `test_editor_lines.c` holds it against a count-from-the-start reference over 20,000 random insert/delete/lookup steps, which is what catches a missed one — the hand-written cases did not. `editor_lines.c` is host-testable with no LCD, like `editor_search.c`. **Found and filed [B32](bugs.md), open, high**: a procedure body over 254 lines re-runs a few of its statements (one extra at 255, one more per ~270 lines), which is nothing to do with the editor — the same body handed straight to `proc_define_from_text` does it — but a 256 KB buffer is what makes 255-line procedures reachable at all. Clean under ASan and UBSan, and **build-sensitive**: identical source failed in one host build directory and passed in another, so the test here stays under 250 body lines and the bug report says not to trust a green run. 77/77 green, `pico+2w` links at 91.19 % RAM and `pico2` at 92.52 %. **Not yet verified on hardware** — the size, the fallback and the line arithmetic are all covered on the host, but the thing the user will judge (typing latency deep in a 100 KB file) has only been reasoned about |

The keymap gives Up and Down an alternate (`KEY_PAGE_UP`/`KEY_PAGE_DOWN`, which is why Shift + Up/Down already page) but leaves Left and Right with none, so **the MCU sends nothing at all** for a shifted horizontal arrow. So `keyboard_poll_once` now folds the latched *ctrl* into the two horizontal arrows, giving `KEY_WORD_LEFT`/`KEY_WORD_RIGHT` (0xB8/0xB9); the raw codes behind the `keydown?` bitmap are built before the fold, so no game sees a change, and `input.c` ignores codes it has no case for. The scan itself is two loops over blanks and non-blanks in `editor_lines.c` rather than `editor.c`, because that file compiles on the host — a word is a run of non-blank characters and a newline is a blank, so a word move crosses lines the way the arrows do and a Ctrl+B selection extends across the jump. **The lesson worth keeping** is that the keymap is the authority on which modifier+key pairs exist at all, and it is two tables in `keyboard.ino` — so the firmware is now written up in [keyboard-firmware-notes.md](keyboard-firmware-notes.md), which also records that the `CFG` register that would disable the substitution is not reachable over I²C, and that Shift + Space and Shift + Backspace are swallowed the same way. 77/77 green, `pico+2w` links at 91.19 % RAM (unchanged). |

It was still matching. **The bug was in the reasoning, which is why it survived a design review, a build and 80 green suites**: §16.2 and §16.6 both argued that refusing `*` on a group "removes catastrophic backtracking by construction rather than by a guard", and that is simply not what causes it — backtracking explodes on *sequential* quantifiers, no nesting required. That gap was not merely theoretical: the reference's own `do.while` example was written `[:call <= 3]`, which raises `I don't know what to do with pr` on a real board (B40), and it had been in the manual unrun. `notequal?` is the negation of `values_equal`, so it inherits the case-insensitive word and element-wise list rules verbatim; the four numeric ones share one `compare_numbers` helper taking which of `<`, `=`, `>` outputs true, which is the whole implementation in nine lines and gives `less?`/`greater?` semantics identical to the `<`/`>` operators in `apply_binary_op` — same `value_to_number` coercion, so a quoted numeral works, and the same `ERR_DOESNT_LIKE_INPUT` on a word or list. **The help-coverage test is what made the documentation mandatory rather than optional**: `help_data.c` is generated from the reference Markdown by `scripts/generate_help.sh`, and `test_primitive_help_coverage` fails the build for any registered primitive with no `##` section — so all ten spellings went into the manual before the suite could go green, and `help "lessequal?` answers on a board. Six sections added (`notequal?` in Words and Lists, the four numeric ones in Arithmetic Operations), the "Difference from other Logo interpreters" note now says outright that there are no `<=`/`>=` operators and names the primitives to use instead, and every documented example output was run through the host REPL rather than written by hand. **Asked to confirm the predicates refuse non-numeric inputs, they did — but the probe found that `value_to_number` did not (B41)**: `sum "0x10 1` output 17 and `sum "nan 1` output `nan`, because the word branch was `strtof` plus "did it consume the whole string", and `strtof` parses C's numerals rather than Logo's. The fix was chosen to be **global rather than local to the new predicates**, at the user's direction: the correct scanner already existed as `is_number_string` but sat in `eval_expr.c` where `value.c` could not reach it, so it moved down into `value.c` and `value.h` and now serves the lexer and the converter alike — one numeral grammar instead of two drifting apart, which is the same split that produced B10. All 80 suites stayed green through it, which is the evidence that nothing depended on the loose forms. A NaN also survives the tightening (`pwr -1 0.5` makes one), and it caught a defect in the new helper before it shipped: `compare_numbers` had inferred equality from "neither less nor greater", so `lessequal?` and `greaterequal?` answered `true` for a NaN where `<`, `>` and `=` all answer `false`. 11 new tests, both new ones checked against the pre-fix code, 80/80 suites green, RAM unmoved at 91.25 %. **Checked on a board** — the ten spellings, the refusals and `help "lessequal?` all behave as designed on a Pico Plus 2 W |
| 2026-08-19 | Cheap wins | **`sort` — a list of words in order, numeric when they all are; a word's characters in ASCII order.** Requested by the user. Ordering a list previously meant writing an insertion sort in Logo, which is a slow thing to interpret and an odd thing to have to write. The list branch collects the elements into a scratch array (the `shuffle` precedent), decides the comparator once by asking `value_to_number` of every element, and `qsort`s: numeric when every word parses as a number, otherwise `strcmp` — deliberately the same ASCII-value, case-sensitive order [`before?`](../reference/Pico_Logo_Reference.md#before-beforep) uses, chosen with the user so the two never disagree about which word comes first. A single non-numeric word switches the whole list to the lexicographic order, so `sort [9 10 a]` outputs `[10 9 a]`. Each entry carries its original index as the comparator's tiebreak, making the sort stable, which is what decides `[1.0 1]` — numerically equal, textually not. **A word input sorts its characters**, matching `reverse` and `shuffle` (the first draft took a list only; the user corrected it): that branch reuses their stack-buffer-or-heap-plus-`mem_word` shape so a >255-char blob sorts too, and it needs no numeric/lexicographic choice at all, since single digits ascend in ASCII order anyway. The one thing `sort` refuses is a list holding a sublist — it has no place in either ordering — which raises `sort doesn't like [1 2 [5 6] 3 9] as input`. Reference section added after `## shuffle` (the help-coverage test requires it, and `help "sort` now answers on a board), 14 new tests, 80/80 suites green, and the pico2 image links with RAM unmoved at 92.58 % (the scratch array is per-call, so it costs flash only) |
| 2026-08-20 | Platform | **The arena/editor balance on boards without PSRAM, rebalanced against what each board can actually open.** Requested by the user, on the principle that *there is no sense in having the ability to run large programs if they cannot be written or edited* — and the numbers agreed. Measured on a board: `asteroids`, at **101,440 bytes the largest program in the tree**, costs about **31 KB of the 128 KB arena** once loaded (16.3 KB of cells, 15.1 KB of atoms; `nodes` and `atoms` report 24,907 and 17,636 free after `load`), so **76 % of the arena sits idle while the editor could not open a single shipped game** — `invaders` is 25,254 bytes and `galaxian` 29,264, both past the 24 KB the editor had, and that 24 KB was itself what made the board panic at boot ([B44](bugs.md)). **The first half of the rebalance costs nothing at all.** The editor's second buffer was sized to match the first, and it never holds a file: `run_editor_and_process` accumulates **one procedure** in it, resetting at every `to`. The longest procedure anywhere in this tree is **2,850 bytes**, and the longest in a shipped game is 1,601 — so a file's worth of SRAM was reserved to hold a procedure's worth. `LOGO_EDITOR_PROC_BUFFER_SIZE` is now 4,096, the same bound `REPL_MAX_PROC_BUFFER` already puts on a definition typed at the prompt, which is the identical job; the PSRAM tier is untouched, since nothing is won by bounding a 256 KB buffer in a region with megabytes going spare. That freed enough heap to **raise** the edit buffer rather than cut it. **The second half is the trade the user asked for**, and it is deliberately the smallest one that buys the capability: the arena gives up 16 KB on `pico2w` (131,072 → 114,688) and 32 KB on `pico2` (→ 98,304, which needs more because its `LOGO_OP_STACK_DEPTH` of 768 spends 102,408 bytes of static RAM against the other boards' 34,824 — the untaken lever if that arena is ever wanted back), and the edit buffer goes to **32,768 on both**. The size is chosen against the files, not rounded for neatness: 32 KB opens `invaders` and `galaxian`, and the next file up is `trails` at 59,970, so anything between 32 KB and 48 KB buys nothing and costs arena. `trails` and `asteroids` stay PSRAM-only, which is honest — no SRAM budget reaches a 101 KB file. `pico+2w` keeps its full 128 KB arena and its 256 KB PSRAM buffer; only its SRAM *fallback* changed (24,576 → 16,384), because that path runs when `psram_verify` fails and 24,576 did not fit its heap either. **Net effect per board**, startup heap against heap available: `pico2` 46,080 / 74,624, `pico2w` 46,080 / 73,540, `pico+2w` 29,696 / 47,804 on the degraded path — **28,544 / 27,460 / 18,108 free** where before two of the three could not boot. Arena after loading `asteroids` on a `pico2w` drops from ~99.6 KB free to ~83 KB, still two thirds idle. **What is not measured is peak node use during play**: the load figure is solid, the frame loop's high-water mark is not, and the check is `print nodes` inside a running game — if it comes back thin, `LOGO_MEMORY_SIZE` is one preset line. Two tests in `test_primitives_editor.c` pin the buffer split; the heap budget itself is arithmetic against the linked images, since the host allocator has no such limit. 80/80 green |

The atom table is the resource under pressure, not the node pool, and it is at its **hard 32,768-byte ceiling** — set by the 15-bit word reference inside a cell (`CELL_WORD_MASK`), which **no preset can raise**. Trying to confirm the arena side on the host turned up why nobody had: `LOGO_MEMORY_SIZE` and `LOGO_EDITOR_BUFFER_SIZE` were wired into the **device** `logo_core` only. The host and test branches each declare their own target (three `add_library(logo_core ...)` in `CMakeLists.txt`) and carried neither, so the cache variable was accepted and silently ignored and four different arena sizes measured **identical** — the tuning knobs for the memory tiers could not be exercised anywhere except on hardware, in a project whose whole method is to measure on the host first. Both are now wired in all three branches. **What that immediately bought**: four host builds at 128/112/96/80 KB, each loading `asteroids`, give free atoms of **17,628 at every one of them** while free nodes fall by exactly the arena difference over four. So the trade is **1:1 in node cells and free in atom bytes**, and the relation is exact — free nodes at arena M is free nodes at 128 KB minus (131,072 − M)/4 — because the two allocators only contend once the node pool grows down as far as `LOGO_ATOM_LIMIT`. Applied to the board's play reading, the shipped `pico2w` (114,688) sees **16,819 free nodes and the same 6,712 atoms** during that minute; asteroids' live set works out at ~3,661 cells against a node capacity of 20,480, a **5.6x margin**. The host figures reproduce the board to within 2 nodes and 8 atom bytes, which is the first time this tree has had a host model of a board's memory it can trust. A `_Static_assert(LOGO_MEMORY_SIZE >= 2 * LOGO_ATOM_LIMIT)` now keeps a preset from cutting the arena so far that the node pool eats the atom ceiling instead — a guard rail, not a proof, since how much node room a workload needs stays a measurement. **The headroom is real enough that a further cut is available and was not taken**: 81,920 would fund a 61,440-byte edit buffer, which reaches `trails` (59,970) and would leave 8,627 free nodes during that same minute — three of the four shipped games editable instead of two, for 33 KB of arena. Held back deliberately, since this tree has already been burned once by a memory margin adopted without measuring the workload it was for (the `reclaim.every` crash, 2026-08-11), and one game measured once is not that workload. **The open risk is atoms and not nodes**: 6,712 free bytes are recycled holes in a table at its ceiling, and `mem_free_atoms` sums them without regard to the largest contiguous run — so a large word could in principle be refused with thousands of bytes 'free'. Unmeasured, and now measurable on the host. **Hardware-accepted 2026-08-20 on the reporting Pico 2 W** — it boots and runs, which closes B44 on the board it was reported from. What that run does *not* settle is the arena half: the smaller node pool was validated by the host model and by arithmetic, not by watching `nodes` inside a frame loop on that board, so the 5.6x margin remains a calculation. 80/80 green |

Both are **averaged over about a second with the peak carried alongside**, which was the play test's second report -- a figure changing fifteen times a second cannot be read off a screen by somebody driving -- and the peak is the half that matters, since it is where a `recycle` spike shows. At 300 MHz the registers still hold the 150 MHz values and **three timings go out of spec at once**: `CLKDIV` 2 drives the PSRAM at **150 MHz against a 133 MHz APS6404**, `RXDELAY` 2 samples a full cycle early where the SDK's own rule adds one above a 100 MHz divided clock, and `MIN_DESELECT` halves in wall-clock to ~10 ns against tCPH 18. Each produces *occasional* bad data rather than a fault, which is the reported signature exactly. It shows in the **editor** and mostly alone because `primitives_editor_init` deliberately puts the edit buffer, the procedure buffer and the vi undo store in PSRAM while the canvas and the cons pool stay in SRAM -- and it is invisible on two boards of three, since only the Plus 2 W has PSRAM, which is why M0's and M1's overclock runs never saw it. **The existing comment predicted the wrong failure for the right reason**: it flagged that `set_sys_clock_khz` leaves QMI timing alone and expected "a hang at the moment of the switch", which is sound for QMI **M0** (flash), where the bootrom's divider is conservative enough that 300 MHz has run whole play tests on both boards. It is **M1**, the PSRAM window, that is over the line. **The fix is written**: `picocalc_retune_psram()`, called from `picocalc_set_cpu_khz` first thing after `set_sys_clock_khz` returns, since going *up* is the only dangerous direction and that window is the whole exposure. It recomputes the four clock-dependent values and writes **one register**, `qmi_hw->m[1].timing` -- deliberately *not* `psram_reinitialize()`, which re-issues QUAD_ENABLE and calls `flash_start_xip()`, an XIP teardown being no way to change a timing field; and because it writes M1 only, flash XIP is untouched and it need not run from RAM. The arithmetic is the SDK's, reproduced because `psram_configure_params` keeps its results in statics it does not export, and **verified bit-identical across 50-400 MHz in 1 MHz steps with every value inside its register field** -- including identical at 150 MHz to what the boot path already programmed, so it is a provable no-op at stock. At 300 MHz it takes the divider 2 -> 3 (SCK 150 -> **100 MHz**), the rx delay 2 -> 3 and the deselect 2 -> 4. **Confirmed fixed on a Pimoroni Pico Plus 2 W the same day.** No host test is possible -- nothing on the host can exercise a QMI register, and the function compiles out entirely on the two boards without PSRAM -- so the fix rests on the arithmetic check above and on the board. Shipped meanwhile: **Battlezone gives the clock back**. It asked for `"fast` at startup and never restored it, so the board stayed overclocked into the editor -- a game that leaves the machine changed rather than merely played on. `restore.clock` puts back **what it found** rather than `"normal`, so a session that was already fast stays fast, and the exit path is checked by reading the tail of `battlezone` out of the source since no test can enter the loop that precedes it. Also this session: the gunsight moved off the horizon -- its two long arms were at y = 40 and `hz` is 40, so they lay *exactly* along the new ground line, and two things drawn on the same row of pixels in two colours are one thing as far as the eye is concerned. The test that guards it checks the **invariant and not the shape**: no stroke may lie on or cross `hz`, which leaves the reticle free to be redrawn without the test having an opinion. 69 battlezone tests, 84/84 ctest green |

What would is the **pivot centre sitting behind the eye** -- a tank rotates about its hull while the periscope is forward of it, so a turn swings the eye sideways, which moves near objects and cannot move something at infinity (~+7 % at 300 steps). | 2026-08-24 | P13 | **Two steering schemes, and the keys that do not move with them** (design §16.8.2). "I need a better control scheme." M2 replaced the arrows with one key per tread and wrote that it RETIRED the M4 question of whether a direct key pair feels better; it did not retire it, it answered it for one player. A tank's controls are the thing people disagree about hardest, so both ship and **`C` on the attract screen chooses**: **arrows** (up/down drive, left/right turn, a forward intent and a turn intent summed into the pair) or **treads** (1/Q and 0/P, M2's). **The clamp is the whole of the arrow scheme** -- forward AND right sums to left 2, right 0, and a tread has three states, so without it the tank drives at double speed whenever it turns; `clamp1` existed for exactly this, went with the arrows at M2 and comes back inline. **It costs one global rather than three**: `tk.r` and `tk.s` are borrowed for the two intents, because `step.tank` writes both the moment `treads` returns, and only `arrows` is new -- which matters at 237 of 254. **And the keys that are not steering no longer move with the scheme**: SPACE fires, **Z** pauses, ESC quits, in both. Space was the pause key and `]` the fire key, a fine layout for hands already on 1/Q and 0/P and a strange one for hands on the arrows -- a control you press without thinking should not depend on a menu you set once. `]` is kept as a second fire key because it is where a right hand on 0/P already is. Five new tests: the arrows drive and steer, the sum clamps on both signs, each scheme ignores the other's keys, fire/pause/quit are checked under BOTH settings rather than the default, and the attract screen's toggle survives `init.game` because the steering is session state. 132 host tests, 84/84 ctest green |

Severity medium: nothing is broken -- every game still makes its budget, Battlezone's peak is 51.7 against 66.7 -- but 23 % of the interpreter's most-used unit is worth about 5 ms of Battlezone's 22 ms body, a third of its headroom |

"We need a volcano with particles, as in the arcade version." `UpdateVolcano` ($58ba) and `DrawVolParts` ($5824) are 90 bytes of ROM and this is a conversion of both: **five slots**, a roll that fills a free one, a random sideways velocity and an upward one, gravity as a decrement on the upward one, death on the ground, and a **point** whose intensity is the top three bits of its own countdown. **One conversion carries all of it and it is the vent.** The ROM's source is 94 units above the horizon and a particle dies at −94, so the fall is exactly the height of the cone; here the crater notch is `item 17 :mtn` = 56 above `hz`, and the ratio **56/94 = 0.6** is spent on the upward velocity and on gravity — 5–12 up becomes 6–14, gravity 1 becomes 2.4 — each doubled again because the cabinet updates at 30 Hz and this frame is 15 (gravity twice, being an acceleration), and the 1-in-8 spawn roll becomes **1-in-4** for the same reason, so the vent throws the same number of sparks *a second*. That arithmetic is checkable rather than plausible: a spark thrown at 14 tops out 41 above the notch and lands after 15 frames, which is where the countdown is set, so **both limits fire** the way the ROM's 31 and its −94 do — and 41 is also what the sky can take, since §6 leaves 120 steps above the horizon and the lip takes 66. **The sideways velocity is the one thing NOT converted**: 1–5 through the same ratio and doubling is 1.2–6, and a spark thrown at 6 clears the lip in seven frames and falls down the *outside* of the cone, reading as coming off the ridge; the mouth is 82 steps wide, so 0.5–2 over at most 15 frames reaches 30 and stays in. **The cone itself is thinner and taller than the first cut** — `mtn` 15–19 is 18, 62, 56, 66, 20, one steep flank each side of the lip–notch–lip, inside 36° where it was a broad rise over 63° — because a wireframe silhouette has no shading to say which slope is the steep one, so width is the only cue it has and a broad one reads as a hill. **And the sparks are white**, not the world's green: they are the one thing in the scene that is not a wireframe object, so they borrow `moon.colour` and cool to the palette's neutral grey — the locked reticle's argument for its red, spent where a colour the tube could not have says something. **The bearing is the horizon table's, not a new number** — the notch is `mtn`'s 17th point and the points are 9° apart, so the vent is 16 · 9 = 144 and the sparks go through the same `k · tan` the ridge line's own vertex is drawn through; the test re-derives the index from `mn.step` rather than repeating 144. **One deviation, and it is the update rather than the draw**: the ROM steps its particles every frame and culls only the drawing, and here the cull covers both, because five sparks stepped and drawn is about **2.7 ms** that would otherwise be spent on the three quarters of headings where the volcano is behind you. The cost is that a spark hangs where it was while you look away — and nothing on screen can show that, because looking away is what makes it true. **Intensity becomes colour**, two levels where the cabinet ramps eight, picked by the countdown and not the height, which is the blip's own trade (§10) in a different currency. **The global table is why the state is shaped the way it is**: §16.12.4 left the peak at 238 of 254 with 16 budgeted for the rest of the workspace, so the particles are **five fields a spark in ONE flat list** mutated with `.setitem` (which allocates nothing), every temporary is `local` rather than L0.5's globals, and the moon gave up both of its names to pay for the list — `mt.m2` folded into `mt.m`, and `mt.m` made local to `moon`. Peak **still 238 of 254**. **And the budget test was under-measuring**, §16.12.4's lesson for the second time: it plays at heading 0, where the moon and the volcano are both off the side, so `draw.moon` never ran and the vent never fired; it now faces each of them once before it reads the peak. Five new tests (**188 in `test_battlezone.c`**), 84/84 ctest green; no firmware source changed |
| 2026-08-26 | P13 | **The sound is read off the disassembly, and the alarm does not survive it** (design §16.14, §16.15). Two passes. The first pins POKEY's clock -- AUDCTL is written `$00`, so `f = 27000 / (AUDF + 1)` and every counter in `sfx_audio_data` is ticked by the 250 Hz NMI at four milliseconds apiece -- and the check on that arithmetic is the 1812 Overture at `$7933`, which decodes to B2 E3 F#3 G#3, each inside half a percent of equal temperament. **The cabinet makes noise in three places**: channels 1 and 2 carry six effects ONE AT A TIME (`StartSoundEffect` is one routine and its callers ask whether the channel is busy, which is what `au.busy` is), channels 3 and 4 carry the missile buzz and nothing else, and discrete hardware at `$1840` carries the engine, the cannon and the explosion as a gate bit and a loud/soft bit with no pitch anywhere. **The missile buzz is the finding** -- AUDF `$ff` and `$fe`, 105.5 against 105.9 Hz, a beating pair M2 had argued out from first principles two milestones before the disassembly arrived. **And M2's proximity alarm does not survive**: the cabinet has a radar ping once a revolution and one three-boop alert the first time an enemy is drawn, and the tempo was telling the player a distance the cabinet only ever showed them. **The second pass was the sequencer spending it wrong**: `voice_advance_block` pops the next note only at `ENV_IDLE`, so a `[0 0 15 15]` envelope added 15 ms to every note in every sequence in the file -- the alert ran 780 ms against 576 -- and each note had been checked and each note was right. A sweep is linear in the divisor and not in pitch, so the sweeps got their steps back. |
| 2026-08-26 | P13 | **Production polish: the development readout goes off, and the procedure table turns out to be full.** The game is finished, and a player is not reading milliseconds: `hud.every` is now **both the averaging period and the switch** -- zero is off, and `play.frame` asks before it tallies -- so rows 25 to 27 are blank in a shipped game and `D` on the attract screen puts the instrument back at fifteen, because every milestone in this design was closed by a board reading those two numbers while somebody drove. **The switch is written as `15 - :hud.every` and that is not cleverness, it is the ceiling**: `MAX_PROCEDURES` is 128 and this file defines **exactly 128**, so the `readout.toggle` that was written first did not fail -- the LAST `to` in the file did, silently, because `proc_define` drops the definition that has no slot. `test_the_game_fits_the_procedure_table` is the guard that names the count, beside the global table's own. Also: 16 over-40-column lines wrapped into the file's own `if`-body idiom (26 → 9, and the nine left are deep-indent arithmetic where wrapping would risk precedence for cosmetics), the stale `M5` banner and the `Battlezone M5` sign-off retired, and 4 new tests (202 host, 84/84 ctest green). |
